From the beginning of the year to the middle of May this year we had more than 25 fatalities compared to the same period last year. This increase is also linked to two tragic bus accidents, one in the state of Styria, where 18 people died alone. But even these two bus accidents with the 20 or 21 dead in total are not justified for the accident figures to rise again. It is necessary and important to achieve, with various measures, that the number of accidents decrease again in the future. A measure which I think is very important is a joint measure by the Ministry of Education and the Interior, which we recently presented and launched, namely a measure on road safety. Last year almost 1,000 accidents occurred on protection routes, 20 people were killed on protection routes. This initiative by the Ministry of Education, the AUVA, as well as the Board of Directors for Transport Safety and the Ministry of the Interior is a contribution to reducing the number of accidents this year. Another contribution is the rigorous examination of the Promille limit. Another contribution is the aggressment of excessive speed, unadapted speed by the executive authorities. For one must be aware of us: Nearly 40 percent of all road deaths are due to accidents due to excessive or unadapted speed!

ParlaMint-AT_1999-05-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00171_d7e5383	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! Estimated colleague Farnleitner! Employment is the basis of social wealth and thus also the basis for the well-developed social system in Austria, which is based on the principle of a solidarity. For social solidarity guarantees inner peace, reliable economic conditions, political stability and thus also the future of democracy. I am deeply convinced that the Austrian path and the development of a social model will prove to be an advantage to Europe in global competition and not a weakness of Europe. The ability to integrate and promote the internal cohesion of a society will become a highly valued location in an increasingly inconsistency and complex society, thus also increasing the competitiveness of Austria. I would also like to stress, however, that this social foundation is necessary to eliminate social division, exclusion, social injustice, violence and xenophobia. I am in favour of this principle of labour orientation and I assume that it will also be the financial basis for our social protection systems in the next millennium. Therefore, we must pay all our attention to enabling as many people as possible to have employment or to participate in working life – and that under social and labour law conditions. At this point, let me expressly say: I do not understand that some can benefit from tax evasions and tax evasions, by circumventing labour and social security legislation. I will continue to fight against it! [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] Dear ladies and gentlemen! However, I would also like to say that I regret the fact that the Government's proposal, which has been sent to the High House under the law on the fight against unemployment, has not yet been dealt with by the Social Committee. Active employment policy to prevent and combat unemployment has always been a priority for the Austrian Federal Government and will continue to be it in the future. With the National Employment Action Plan, we have now made this objective binding and concrete, and I will allow it to be implemented in a short time, we have also made substantial progress. However, it must also be made clear that European and Austrian employment policies must go hand in hand and that it is not possible to decouple national employment policies from pan-European trends and developments. That is why we have been committed to the creation of the employment guidelines at European level, and we have also made a special effort to develop these guidelines within the meaning of a European employment strategy during the Austrian Presidency – and that, as I believe, is successful. The decision to draw up National Action Plans for Employment has given the Member States of the European Union the obligation to formulate concrete and measurable objectives for the creation of additional employment and the reduction of unemployment, and also a continuous observation – I would like to say almost: Control – to undergo. As part of the National Action Plan for Employment, which was first adopted by the Federal Government in April 1998 with the involvement of the social partners, the European Union's employment guidelines will be implemented together with a commitment taken over by all the Member States of the European Union. Now, after the first year of the National Action Plan and in the light of the 1999 plan's adaptation to the more developed guidelines, we can draw up an interim review of the first experience with the NAP as a basis for employment policy objectives. It is clear from this that the National Action Plan on Employment Policy in Austria has given important new impetus and has also begun a new era in combating unemployment. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] This finding, ladies and gentlemen, can also be supported by facts. In the first year of the NAP, the number of self-employed workers could be increased by 30 000. The increase in women's employment in particular is another positive aspect. After all, at the end of 1998, around 16 300 self-employed women could be counted more than at the end of 1997. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] [[Intrusion of the Abg. Böhacker.]] These additional employment conditions also include part-time workers, but in the full-time equivalence we are primarily within the European Union. I do not want to see it stigmatized, too, in the sense of part-time employment. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP as well as the Liberal Forum.]] Dear ladies and gentlemen! More recently, the Wifo reported a seasonally adjusted increase in employees by 18 000 in the first four months of 1999 and was also ready to revise clearly the original forecast that only a stagnant of unemployment figures had predicted for this year. After all, the national registry rate has fallen from 7.2 percent in April 1998 to 7 percent in April 1999. The number of unemployed has fallen by almost 10,000 compared to the previous year. I believe that this is a verifiable and comprehensible success of a common employment policy. It is clear that, in addition to the encouraging economic situation – of course, it is primarily the economy which provides jobs which give the opportunity for employment through growth – the importance which the Federal Government has attached to employment policy has made a significant contribution and that this has made additional measures. Let us take, for example, the budgetary provisions in the field of labour market policies, which are to be taken into account by my department: The financial resources spent by Austria for active and active labour market policies were increased by no less than 36 percent in 1999, i.e. from 8.2 billion schilling to 11.1 billion schilling in the first budget year following the NAP decision. In this context, the main focus was on the integrative approach to re-implementing what is called passive cash benefits in order not to endanger fiscal consolidation. Dear ladies and gentlemen! I am pleased to note that, with our share of active budget for employment policy, we are now in the middle of expenditure on active employment measures within the OECD countries. Allow me briefly to point out the essential innovations, accents and adaptations of the 1999 NAP. The key pillars of the 1999 guidelines are still: better employability and skills for workers – that is, the so-called pillar 1 –, promoting entrepreneurship – that is the pillar 2, increasing the adaptability of enterprises and their employees to changing economic conditions – that is the Pillar 3 – and equal opportunities on the labour market – which is the Pillar 4. Austria has already stressed the special link between economic policy and employment in the National Action Plan 1998 and, in addition to an appropriate growth policy, has in particular referred to technology and export promotion, infrastructure investment, business settlements and the fight against the shadow economy as important elements of an active employment policy. Under the Austrian EU Presidency last year, while maintaining the structure of the guidelines, new emphasis has been placed on some important points. In particular, this concerns upgrading and broadening the policy of equal opportunities for women and men, the so-called gender mainstreaming. Under the new Guideline No. 19, all measures taken under the NAP should also review their availability in terms of improving equal opportunities. It is therefore intended to remove obstacles to women's employment and effectively combat the division of the labour market between men and women. – This is the guideline 20 which has been addressed here. There are also innovations in the field of lifelong learning. We have opted for the concept of life-long learning, because life-long learning may be a threat and not a positive one. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] We have set out in Guideline 6 concrete guidelines for increasing participation in continuing training measures and therefore we have very clear criteria. The new Guideline 4 calls for specific measures for older workers. The preliminary work on the implementation of the "Pact for older workers" presented by the social partners is in progress, and I hope that in a few weeks we will be able to present you with a concrete negotiation outcome and a legal initiative. The government template for tax reform has already been sent to you, including measures relating to the tax and tax system, which is intended to be employment-friendly. The new Guideline 13 calls on Member States to develop framework conditions in order to fully unlock the employment potential of the information society and the environment. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! In the conclusions of the Vienna European Council, the so-called Vienna Strategy was established. It sees macroeconomic components as an essential part of a successful employment strategy, covering both economic policy coordination and tax policy, international financial structure, monetary policy and infrastructure investment. However, it also stresses the principle that all actors should pursue a coordinated policy with the involvement of the social partners and the European Central Bank. These macroeconomic aspects are gaining special importance in view of the reduced growth prospects in Europe. The promotion of an employment-oriented growth policy will therefore be an important prerequisite for meeting the ambitious objectives of the guidelines. For me, it is crucial for the European and Austrian employment strategies that the Cologne European Council, which will take place in June, will bring further progress and further concreteisations in this direction, such as a genuine political agreement between all decision-makers from economic, financial and employment policy, with the clear commitment to promoting growth and employment, while respecting the stability criteria. Dear ladies and gentlemen! In the NAP 1999 you will also find a new objective, namely to increase the R & D rate to 2.5 percent by 2005 and to provide additional resources to achieve this objective as early as 1999. . I recall the nightly discussion, during which we have already been able to discuss this quota in detail. – This will increase product and process innovation as well as the competitiveness of the economy and thus the chance of new jobs. A critical point of the Austrian labour market is still high seasonal unemployment. In the construction and tourism sector in particular, we are required to improve this situation through continuous public investment and the development of working time and mediation models. Dear ladies and gentlemen! However, it is also necessary to develop and secure the strengths of Austrian labour market policy. This includes a new edition of the Youth Employment Programme, together with the tax promotions of youth employment decided under the tax reform, as well as training already available in employment. However, this also includes targeted support for mobility through activation programmes during unemployment, such as through job coaching. The promotion of women's employment, in particular by removing the obstacles to re-establishment after family interruptions and by increasing the level of childcare assistance, is another important emphasis of the NAP in 1999 and is also clearly quantified. Dear ladies and gentlemen! Therefore, in the event of a corresponding development in the economic environment, especially in the case of economic growth, it will be possible to achieve an increase in employment by around 100 000 by 2002 and a reduction of the unemployment rate to about 3.5 percent in 2002 within the five-year timeframe of the NAP. However, it is also necessary that, as provided for in the National Action Plan, we provide the capacity to bring 20 percent of the job-seekers into active action. If this, just because it appears to be opportunist on a daily basis, is put down as "beautiful of statistics" or even as "hidden of the unemployed", then those who do so ignore the sustainable success of these measures, but they also ignore the needs of people seeking employment and are on the wrong side. [[Congratulations with SPÖ and ÖVP.]] It is therefore evident, ladies and gentlemen, that we are on the right, successful path with the National Action Plan for Employment and the whole of our Austrian employment policy. – I would like to thank you for the support you have provided in this way and also for your further support.

We have also tried to include regional funding in this report. There is a region that is defined by the common language, but one is forced to use state boundaries as a competition line or a competitive mechanism. ParlaMint-AT_1999-05-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00171_d7e18000	Thank you very much, Mr State Secretary, for your comprehensive statements. Mrs Moser-Starrach has now been reported to the House. 5 minutes of voluntary time limit. Please, ladies and gentlemen.
ParlaMint-AT_1999-05-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00171_d7e18025	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! High house! Mr State Secretary, you have now answered the question of why the folk operal orchestra is so badly paid, but you have the ball, actually the cannon ball, in the folk opera. If even the frog in the premiere brings on stage that poor folk operal orchestra is so badly paid, we may have to do something in this direction. However, I would like to refer to the Government template 1705 of the Supplements, the Agreement between the Government of the Republic of Austria and the Government of the Russian Federation on Cultural Cooperation. With this agreement between the Government of the Republic of Austria and the Government of the Russian Federation on cultural cooperation, the National Council has long since passed a bilateral cultural agreement for approval. The larger part of the cultural agreements which are binding on Austria today already dates back to the 1970s. At that time, the cultural agreements were a widely appreciated instrument of foreign culture policy and a strengthening of general relations with certain countries – an instrument which was also used successfully by Austria under the appropriate conditions, both in relation to other European countries and to the individual developing countries and also in relation to those States which were subject to the then communist power. The structure of these contracts was the same on a regular basis. It consisted essentially of the establishment of the various areas of cooperation and the establishment of a mixed cultural commission with the respective contracting party, which had to advise and decide on multiannual work programmes at periodic intervals on the basis of the Agreement. In the 1980s, the tendency to view the instrument of bilateral cultural agreements as no longer modern and to distance itself from the conclusion of new agreements of this kind increased. The main argument for this was and continues to be that cross-border cultural relations are increasingly or largely outside of the state influence and that the instrument of the classical bilateral cultural agreements in this environment has therefore become inactive and unnecessary. This argument, of course, is aimed primarily at the cross-border cultural relations between Western countries, but after the turn of 1989 it was also used in terms of cultural relations with the former communist states in Central and Eastern Europe. However, experience in recent years has shown that, under certain conditions with certain partners, the instrument of the classical bilateral cultural agreement can continue to fulfil an important task in the context of foreign culture policy. The first concerns western and southern European states, such as France, Italy, Portugal and Spain, which, like Austria, have an active foreign culture policy and already appreciate the instruments of the classical bilateral cultural agreement, because it offers the institutional opportunity to take up a periodic inventory with the partner country and discuss operational issues, such as the cultural institutions active in the partner country. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] The implementation of the existing cultural agreements with those countries has been consistently pursued, and the current work programmes apply here. Secondly, we are dealing with the countries of Central and Eastern Europe mentioned earlier. In these countries, as in Austria, we find the situation that the budgetary security of certain services in the context of these cultural relations depends on the existence of a jointly agreed work programme based on a cultural agreement. The interest of the partner countries in the further implementation of the cultural agreements concluded with Austria is quite clear. There, where the respective design of the succession of states has removed an earlier integration into a cultural agreement with Austria, including Slovakia and Slovenia, negotiations on new bilateral cultural agreements were sought and subsequently conducted. In relation to Russia, it was agreed in 1994 that the 1968 cultural agreement concluded with the Soviet Union at the time would continue to apply. However, there was no comprehensive application, in particular there were no longer regular meetings and agreements on the implementation of work programmes. It is to be hoped that in the first programme of work prepared on the basis of the new agreement, the Russian side will be ready to grant multiple-entry visas to Austrian readers and cultural experts. In conclusion, my group sees the agreement as meaningful and useful and will agree with it. In this context, the question arises whether it would not be appropriate to extend the network of bilateral cultural agreements concluded by Austria to new partners under certain positive conditions, i.e. to those not already involved in a cultural agreement. I could imagine that a State like the People's Republic of China could strengthen and strengthen bilateral cultural relations if they were structured by a bilateral cultural agreement.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-05-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00171_d7e18777	Mr Krüger has also been mentioned on the floor. Please, honourable Member.
ParlaMint-AT_1999-05-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00171_d7e18799	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! It is certainly logical – and that is why the Group of the Liberal, Democratic and Reformist Party agrees – that in the context of the third pillar of the European Court of Justice, it must be decided in cases of doubt if the national tribunal, the national court, under the third pillar, makes an application for a preliminary ruling. I should like to draw attention to the legal experience that we have had with the preliminary rulings of the European Court of Justice in the field of civil justice. The experience is not bad, however, it is to be complained that the time needed by the European Court of Justice to make a preliminary ruling is very long. I can document this on the basis of an example: An Austrian company has established an Austrian brand for years. There is also a European company that has also established a brand in almost all the Member States of the European Union, but not in Austria. This company now wants to build a European brand and, at least in Austria, meets with the resistance of that brand that has existed here before and points to its priority. The Austrian company filed the complaint and a request for an interim order against this international group, which suddenly appears here with the same brand, because it is operating under this flagship throughout Europe. In the specific case, the interlocutory order was rejected at first instance, the second instance confirmed. The Supreme Court of Justice has followed the revision review and has given a preliminary ruling against the international group – in this case, Tengelmann – on failure to use this word mark here in Austria. In the ordinary proceedings, in the so-called main proceedings – Mr Wittmann knows what I am talking about – the case has naturally returned to the Supreme Court, and the Supreme Court has referred the question of the priority of a European trade mark to the European Court of Justice vis-à-vis a regional, national and national trade mark. The European Court of Justice has taken a very positive decision on behalf of the national company, which has been welcomed. The European Court of Justice is also responsible for setting up smaller companies, which have only a national significance, against international groups in Austria. However, it must be lamented that this procedure really takes too long, in the case of concrete two years. If one considers that this is only a preliminary ruling in the context of a complete court procedure and that the course of action, including the preliminary ruling procedure, is then a total of more than five or six years, then it is unacceptable. There is a need to think of this in order to shorten it, and to put pressure on it in the European Union.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-05-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00171_d7e20916	The next speaker is Mr Magistrate. Merchant. 5 minutes of voluntary time limit. Please, honourable Member.
ParlaMint-AT_1999-05-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00171_d7e20937	Mr President. High house! At this first reading, it is first of all a matter of addressing this problem in principle. I have just three brief comments on this: First: I am in principle in favour of the law being passed as Mrs Hostasch has sent out as a draft. This means that, in the case of works council elections, there is a passive right of election for foreigners, when there is a license of liberation, that is, after five years. Secondly, Mr Oellinger, I think that your statement in the request is actually wrong. It is certainly not possible to use the Federal Constitutional Act to eliminate all forms of racial discrimination as a reason, otherwise you would have been able to challenge all workers' chamber elections and all works council elections since 1973. Obviously, this argument does not stand. Thirdly, It is clear that, in addition to Austrian citizens, all EEA citizens now have the passive right to vote. This is the result of the entire regulatory complex of Austrian legislation and EU legislation, even if this passive right of election is not explicitly included in the Chamber of Workers' Election Law or in the Constitutional Labour Act. The question is whether citizens with whom an association agreement has been concluded have this passive right to vote. At the end of the day, this depends on how to qualify "other working conditions" because they are equal under these agreements in terms of wages, salaries and other working conditions. "Other working conditions" certainly do not cover the passive right to vote when it comes to the legislative body, i.e. the Chamber of Workers. The passive right to vote can very well be included when it comes to a more direct choice such as the choice of works councils in a company. I therefore believe that what Mrs Hostasch has sent to evaluate would be absolutely a good basis, which could be decided in this form. – Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-AT_1999-05-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00171_d7e21664	Mr President. High house! I would have hoped that such a legislative proposal regarding a change to the labour market service law would not be necessary. Unfortunately, it is not. The AMS, a newly created body that has only just replaced the former labour market administration for a few years, i.e. a body created in the 1990s, has a scandalous low female share in senior positions. The political parties, the social partners, the trade union, the workers' chambers, the economic chambers: they are the ones who order these functions. I am therefore referring, in particular, to social democracy, which is in itself a programme for equality, and which understands the question: Why do you allow that institution, which is responsible for the labour market issues of all Austrians, to have such a scandalous low female share? Is it really necessary for us to act by law here? - I'm afraid so. I tell you what it really looks like with women in executive functions: The board consists of two men – a red one, a black one, of course, in Austria. Of the nine national business leaders and the nine alternates, i.e. 18 persons, 17 men and a woman are allowed to advise in Vienna. That means that we already have 20 management functions, of which only one is a woman as a deputy, that is, 5 percent. That is, even union and workers' chamber did not send a corresponding proportion of women to the AMS. At the Board of Directors it is better a nuance: Of the 18 members, 15 men and 3 women are "only" – under quotation marks. This means that of the 38 management positions in total, a total of 4 are women, and these are mostly alternates and not officials. This is what the situation looks like, and I am convinced that precisely for this reason the completely uneven occupation – with the tolerance of social democracy – does not really work satisfactorily in the area of women's promotion. I am very glad that there was additional money for the promotion of apprentices. That was okay. There one should also check which companies and who gets this, in which channels it flows, how high the administrative share is and how much money really benefits those affected. But in principle it is right to do something for the apprentices. ParlaMint-AT_1999-05-20-020-XX-NRSITZ-00171_d7e22026	Mr President. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! The Austrian Federal Government would have had 14th day. That is why we have presented our own draft to the liberals. This draft contains, as a key point, one of all the administrative simplifications desired, namely that the commercial authority is EIA authorities at the same time. This means, in turn, that this authority is the filing authority for all investment-related procedures. To this end, there are set deadlines which generally lead to a reduction in the time limits which are still relatively long today and which are to be implemented relatively quickly. There is also a clearly defined list of EIA-based procedures. The responsibility lies exclusively with the Minister for the Environment, the execution is with the national leaders. With the adoption of a positive decision, when it comes to our proposal, implementing powers will be transferred to the matter authorities. Citizens' participation is regulated in such a way that everyone has the right to understand and to advise, that citizens' initiatives have an extended right of consultation and that they are represented to the authorities by their legally legitimate representatives, such as environmental lawyers. Ladies and gentlemen! This is, as I believe, a proposal which is quite in line with what is required by the EU and on the one hand by the economy on the other, but also in line with what environmental organisations imagine. It would, of course, make us very happy if this request were to be reflected at least in part in the work of the administration, it should finally be able to submit a draft accordingly.

There are some points in the Freedom request, which are certainly interesting, although some of them are not largely enough from my point of view. For example, the possibility of considering the documents is not yet a genuine citizen's participation, as many citizens' initiatives and environmental organisations expect from a novel. All in all, I would have hoped that we would have done so on 10 October. In June 1999, it was possible to debate, including, of course, the request of the liberals.
